Pass the interpolated value to `printf` as an ARGUMENT — `printf("n=%d", n)` — which is the same text and is what this path lowers; `print("n=", n)` is NOT the same program, because `print` inserts a space between its operands, and building the text with `+` is the same missing buffer refused under another name.)

CODEGEN: formal/memcheck/local_uninitialised.mojo  (build: main: 'a' is read at line 24 before anything in this function stores it, and CPython raises UnboundLocalError for that program (NameError at module level). CODEGEN: t_argv.mojo  (build: main: sys.argv reads 'argv' out of the imported module `sys`, and a module is not a value this path can place: there is no register, frame slot or `__DATA` word for it because it is not one — it is the library on this link line, and what a dylib publishes is its FUNCTIONS (as symbols, so `sys.fn(...)` lowers) and its module-level names the build FOLDED TO A LITERAL (as values, so `sys.K = 1` lowers — there is exactly one value of a folded module-level name in a whole program, so the importer materializes the same one). `sys` publishes no 'argv', so there is nothing on the other side of the boundary to read — which is a DIFFERENT fact from "it is there and cannot cross", and the two have different repairs: a name the module's own functions write through `global` gets the sentence above, which names an accessor that works today, and a name it declares as a container gets the one above that, which names an element accessor. What `sys` publishes: api_version, byteorder, flush_output, get_coroutine_origin_tracking_depth, get_int_max_str_digits, getdefaultencoding, getfilesystemencodeerrors, getfilesystemencoding, …. `formal/hostmods/sys.mojo`'s own module docstring says which of those names it deliberately does not declare and why (`argv`, `stderr`, `stdout`, `modules`, `path`), and `FORMAL_module_state_no_storage.md (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted)` §(2) and §(4) record what would have to be true to close the boundary for the ones CPython has and this path does not)

A string here is a bare `char *` interned into read+execute __TEXT, so `"n=" + decimal(n)` has nowhere to be laid down at compile time (the field may be a runtime value) or at run time (there is no heap); this is the same missing buffer that keeps string concatenation and the length-dependent methods refused (see `string_concat_refusal` and LENGTH_DEPENDENT_METHODS). Pass the interpolated value to `printf` as an ARGUMENT — `printf("n=%d", n)` — which is the same text and is what this path lowers; `print("n=", n)` is NOT the same program, because `print` inserts a space between its operands, and building the text with `+` is the same missing buffer refused under another name.)

A string here is a bare `char *` interned into read+execute __TEXT, so `"n=" + decimal(n)` has nowhere to be laid down at compile time (the field may be a runtime value) or at run time (there is no heap); this is the same missing buffer that keeps string concatenation and the length-dependent methods refused (see `string_concat_refusal` and LENGTH_DEPENDENT_METHODS). Pass the interpolated value to `printf` as an ARGUMENT — `printf("n=%d", n)` — which is the same text and is what this path lowers; `print("n=", n)` is NOT the same program, because `print` inserts a space between its operands, and building the text with `+` is the same missing buffer refused under another name.)

The parser keeps the whole source token (`f'/Users/mrs/net/chatgpt/claude/{checkout}/{rel}'`) as the literal's value, so what this build would have printed is the spelling: with `n = 7`, `print(f"n={n}")` printed `f"n={n}"` and exited 0 where CPython prints `n=7`. A string here is a bare `char *` interned into read+execute __TEXT, so `"n=" + decimal(n)` has nowhere to be laid down at compile time (the field may be a runtime value) or at run time (there is no heap); this is the same missing buffer that keeps string concatenation and the length-dependent methods refused (see `string_concat_refusal` and LENGTH_DEPENDENT_METHODS). Pass the interpolated value to `printf` as an ARGUMENT — `printf("n=%d", n)` — which is the same text and is what this path lowers; `print("n=", n)` is NOT the same program, because `print` inserts a space between its operands, and building the text with `+` is the same missing buffer refused under another name.)

What would remove it is not a declaration this file is missing, and saying so is the point: the two things that would answer it are a value model for a container-valued module constant — inline it at the use site, which needs storage this path does not have (`(module state/model limits; cross-module accessors for writable globals) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted; fixed by cross-module accessors for writable globals)`) — and a BACKEND rule that a module nothing binds needs no library at all, an importer reading the constant directly instead of linking a library for it (`bugs/FORMAL_a_module_that_exports_nothing_cannot_be_a_dylib.md`). Neither exists here, so this is refused at the build rather than linked as a library with nothing in it, and nothing here is waiting on this file.)

The parser keeps the whole source token (`f"{a!r} != {b!r}"`) as the literal's value, so what this build would have printed is the spelling: with `n = 7`, `print(f"n={n}")` printed `f"n={n}"` and exited 0 where CPython prints `n=7`. A string here is a bare `char *` interned into read+execute __TEXT, so `"n=" + decimal(n)` has nowhere to be laid down at compile time (the field may be a runtime value) or at run time (there is no heap); this is the same missing buffer that keeps string concatenation and the length-dependent methods refused (see `string_concat_refusal` and LENGTH_DEPENDENT_METHODS). Pass the interpolated value to `printf` as an ARGUMENT — `printf("n=%d", n)` — which is the same text and is what this path lowers; `print("n=", n)` is NOT the same program, because `print` inserts a space between its operands, and building the text with `+` is the same missing buffer refused under another name.)

CODEGEN: test_regress_callable_ctype_param.py  (build: test_callable_ctype_parameterized: 'types' is imported from `mojo.middle`, and it is a module-level name of another module. This path compiles an import into a dylib, and a dylib publishes FUNCTIONS and folded CONSTANTS — a name whose value the build knows (`mojo.middle.K = 1`, `mojo.middle.S = "x"`, a `comptime` constant) lowers, and a call `mojo.middle.fn(...)` lowers. What it cannot publish is a VARIABLE: there is no storage for one here, because every value a formal program can name lives in a function's own stack scratch, which is reclaimed when the function returns — and a list, a stream object or a function used as a value is one of those, not a constant. So this name's value is a real global with nowhere to live, which is a property of the value model rather than of this call: `mojo.middle.fn(...)` where `mojo.middle` publishes that function as a function is the same program with a representation. Pass the interpolated value to `printf` as an ARGUMENT — `printf("n=%d", n)` — which is the same text and is what this path lowers; `print("n=", n)` is NOT the same program, because `print` inserts a space between its operands, and building the text with `+` is the same missing buffer refused under another name.)

CODEGEN: test_relaxed_imports.mojo  (build: '+' on two strings is refused on this path. NOT-ANSWERABLE/TARGET-LIMIT: test_sqlite3.mojo  (build: mojo_sqlite3_open is an entry point of the gimple backend's C runtime (the `mojo_*` ABI declared in runtime/*.h), and a formal image is freestanding: it links libSystem, whatever modules it imports, and nothing of runtime/ unless it names an entry point here — in which case `formal/build.py` puts the per-architecture `mojo_*` library on the link line for it. Its values are one 64-bit word. It could not be answered by linking that library either: its return value is `void *`, and that is an address rather than a value. An ARGUMENT can be one: this path carries the word and hands it straight back to the library that made it, never looking at what is behind it. A return is the other way round — the caller is the one who would have to read it, and the declaration does not say what is there. `void *` is spelled the same for a handle a program only passes on and for a container it reads elements out of, and a `MojoStr *` is a struct in the C heap, which a freestanding image has no allocator for. Either way the answer is a box, and it is not a box this path can produce. Refused rather than emitted as a call to a symbol nothing defines, which is what this used to do — the image built and then died in the loader with "Symbol not found".)

The parser keeps the whole source token (`f"{a!r} != {b!r}"`) as the literal's value, so what this build would have printed is the spelling: with `n = 7`, `print(f"n={n}")` printed `f"n={n}"` and exited 0 where CPython prints `n=7`. Pass the interpolated value to `printf` as an ARGUMENT — `printf("n=%d", n)` — which is the same text and is what this path lowers; `print("n=", n)` is NOT the same program, because `print` inserts a space between its operands, and building the text with `+` is the same missing buffer refused under another name.)

What `os` publishes: F_OK, R_OK, W_OK, X_OK, access, chdir, chmod, cpu_count, …. `formal/hostmods/sys.mojo`'s own module docstring says which of those names it deliberately does not declare and why (`argv`, `stderr`, `stdout`, `modules`, `path`), and `FORMAL_module_state_no_storage.md (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted)` §(2) and §(4) record what would have to be true to close the boundary for the ones CPython has and this path does not)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/_gpu/_utils.mojo  (build: _utils.mojo imports 'std.collections.string.string_span', which cannot be built either: Slice.write_to: `FormatStruct` is called, and it is imported from `std.format._utils`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.format._utils` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule applied to the DECLARATION: a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation, and each instantiation is the boundary symbol — `formal/monomorph.py` compiles one into the defining module's library when an importer asks for it, and a bare call asks for none. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/_plugin/selector.mojo  (build: selector.mojo imports 'std.sys.info', which cannot be built either: Slice.write_to: `FormatStruct` is called, and it is imported from `std.format._utils`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.format._utils` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule applied to the DECLARATION: a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation, and each instantiation is the boundary symbol — `formal/monomorph.py` compiles one into the defining module's library when an importer asks for it, and a bare call asks for none. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/benchmark/compiler.mojo  (build: inlined_assembly['', NoneType, 'r,~{memory}', True] is a subscript whose index is a tuple. A value here is one 64-bit word and a list is a flat blob of words, so a tuple index has no representation on this path. It is one of two things: a lookup keyed by the tuple, which is lowered only when the base is a known dict and compares the key element-wise; or a two-dimensional index, which needs a row stride the source never states. Refused rather than computing a plausible flat index, and rather than using the tuple blob's own address as the index — that builds, runs, and returns an element nobody asked for.)
CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/benchmark/memory.mojo  (build: memory.mojo imports 'std.atomic', which cannot be built either: Slice.write_to: `FormatStruct` is called, and it is imported from `std.format._utils`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.format._utils` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule applied to the DECLARATION: a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation, and each instantiation is the boundary symbol — `formal/monomorph.py` compiles one into the defining module's library when an importer asks for it, and a bare call asks for none. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)
BACKEND-CRASH: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/benchmark/__init__.mojo  (the backend raised: formal.monomorph.MonomorphError: sqrt[SIMD[dtype,width], 1] is a comptime specialization of a FUNCTION template, and a value specialization of a function is a call site binding, not a boundary symbol: both backends already lower it through `mojo/middle/comptime.specialization_args`, so demanding an instantiation here would publish a second copy of a body the build already emits under its own name.  This is not a refusal of the program — the call keeps its brackets and the local machinery answers them)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/_coroutine.mojo  (build: _coroutine.mojo imports 'std.sys', which cannot be built either: _io.mojo: formal dylib has no public functions: _io.mojo exports nothing under doc/ABI.md's rules because it declares no function and no type at all — only module-level constants, and a constant is not a boundary symbol: there is nothing an importer could bind, so the refusal itself is correct. What would remove it is not a declaration this file is missing, and saying so is the point: the two things that would answer it are a value model for a container-valued module constant — inline it at the use site, which needs storage this path does not have (`(module state/model limits; cross-module accessors for writable globals) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted; fixed by cross-module accessors for writable globals)`) — and a BACKEND rule that a module nothing binds needs no library at all, an importer reading the constant directly instead of linking a library for it (`bugs/FORMAL_a_module_that_exports_nothing_cannot_be_a_dylib.md`). Neither exists here, so this is refused at the build rather than linked as a library with nothing in it, and nothing here is waiting on this file.)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/_stubs.mojo  (build: _stubs.mojo imports 'std.os.os', which cannot be built either: builtin_slice.mojo: is an `Optional[Int]`, and this target has no word to spell `None` as for it: every word is a value of `Int`. An `Optional` here is ONE 64-bit word holding the payload, so `None` has to be a word the payload cannot produce (`formal/model.py`'s `optional_none_word`, which is what answers `Optional[Bool]`, `Optional[String]`, `Optional[Int32]` and every reference-shaped payload from this same table). Answered anyway it would have to treat the integer 0 as the empty one, which is WRONG about `Some(0)` — measured on both architectures: `var z: Optional[Int] = 0; if z is None:` prints the empty branch. So this is refused rather than answered. What closes it is the OTHER representation: a tagged TWO-WORD value, `{tag, payload}`, where `Optional[T]` is the address of the pair — which is what `formal/model.py`'s `OPTIONAL_TWO_WORD` records, with the three measured reasons it is not a one-line change, and which covers every payload type including this one)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/format_int.mojo  (build: format_int.mojo imports 'std.os', which cannot be built either: _Global._deinit_wrapper: `dealloc` is called, and it is imported from `std.memory.alloc`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.memory.alloc` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule applied to the DECLARATION: a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation, and each instantiation is the boundary symbol — `formal/monomorph.py` compiles one into the defining module's library when an importer asks for it, and a bare call asks for none. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.dealloc`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `dealloc(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `dealloc[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/dtype.mojo  (build: dtype.mojo imports 'std.hashlib.hasher', which cannot be built either: _io.mojo: formal dylib has no public functions: _io.mojo exports nothing under doc/ABI.md's rules because it declares no function and no type at all — only module-level constants, and a constant is not a boundary symbol: there is nothing an importer could bind, so the refusal itself is correct. What would remove it is not a declaration this file is missing, and saying so is the point: the two things that would answer it are a value model for a container-valued module constant — inline it at the use site, which needs storage this path does not have (`(module state/model limits; cross-module accessors for writable globals) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted; fixed by cross-module accessors for writable globals)`) — and a BACKEND rule that a module nothing binds needs no library at all, an importer reading the constant directly instead of linking a library for it (`bugs/FORMAL_a_module_that_exports_nothing_cannot_be_a_dylib.md`). Neither exists here, so this is refused at the build rather than linked as a library with nothing in it, and nothing here is waiting on this file.)

CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/len.mojo  (build: a `...` stands where this path needs instructions to emit, and there are none: a formal image is a compiled program, so every function it lowers must have a body. This is the language's own no-implementation marker — write the function, or declare it as a `trait` method, which is a declaration of an interface and so has no body to lower (a `...` in a trait method builds on both architectures, and a `...` in a plain function is refused whether or not anything ever calls it))
CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/none.mojo  (build: writer.write_string() is a method on a Writer — a multi-field struct, so on this path the receiver is the ADDRESS of a frame of 8-byte slots, not a file descriptor. It is not the `write` that is lowered: that one is honest only because open(...) makes its receiver a descriptor, and nothing here establishes that a Writer is one. Lowering it as a write would pass a frame address as fd(2): the syscall returns EBADF, nothing checks it, and the program's output is missing rather than wrong-looking. Refused rather than emitted as a call to a symbol spelled 'writer.write_string', which is what this used to do: the image built and then died in the loader)
CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/debug_assert.mojo  (build: debug_assert.mojo imports 'std._plugin', which cannot be built either: builtin_slice.mojo: is an `Optional[Int]`, and this target has no word to spell `None` as for it: every word is a value of `Int`. An `Optional` here is ONE 64-bit word holding the payload, so `None` has to be a word the payload cannot produce (`formal/model.py`'s `optional_none_word`, which is what answers `Optional[Bool]`, `Optional[String]`, `Optional[Int32]` and every reference-shaped payload from this same table). Answered anyway it would have to treat the integer 0 as the empty one, which is WRONG about `Some(0)` — measured on both architectures: `var z: Optional[Int] = 0; if z is None:` prints the empty branch. So this is refused rather than answered. What closes it is the OTHER representation: a tagged TWO-WORD value, `{tag, payload}`, where `Optional[T]` is the address of the pair — which is what `formal/model.py`'s `OPTIONAL_TWO_WORD` records, with the three measured reasons it is not a one-line change, and which covers every payload type including this one)
BACKEND-CRASH: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/builtin/int_literal.mojo  (the backend raised: formal.monomorph.MonomorphError: sqrt[SIMD[dtype,width], 1] is a comptime specialization of a FUNCTION template, and a value specialization of a function is a call site binding, not a boundary symbol: both backends already lower it through `mojo/middle/comptime.specialization_args`, so demanding an instantiation here would publish a second copy of a body the build already emits under its own name.  This is not a refusal of the program — the call keeps its brackets and the local machinery answers them)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/collections/__init__.mojo  (build: __init__.mojo imports '.dict', which cannot be built either: builtin_slice.mojo: is an `Optional[Int]`, and this target has no word to spell `None` as for it: every word is a value of `Int`. An `Optional` here is ONE 64-bit word holding the payload, so `None` has to be a word the payload cannot produce (`formal/model.py`'s `optional_none_word`, which is what answers `Optional[Bool]`, `Optional[String]`, `Optional[Int32]` and every reference-shaped payload from this same table). Answered anyway it would have to treat the integer 0 as the empty one, which is WRONG about `Some(0)` — measured on both architectures: `var z: Optional[Int] = 0; if z is None:` prints the empty branch. So this is refused rather than answered. What closes it is the OTHER representation: a tagged TWO-WORD value, `{tag, payload}`, where `Optional[T]` is the address of the pair — which is what `formal/model.py`'s `OPTIONAL_TWO_WORD` records, with the three measured reasons it is not a one-line change, and which covers every payload type including this one)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/collections/check_bounds.mojo  (build: check_bounds.mojo imports '.', which cannot be built either: _io.mojo: formal dylib has no public functions: _io.mojo exports nothing under doc/ABI.md's rules because it declares no function and no type at all — only module-level constants, and a constant is not a boundary symbol: there is nothing an importer could bind, so the refusal itself is correct. What would remove it is not a declaration this file is missing, and saying so is the point: the two things that would answer it are a value model for a container-valued module constant — inline it at the use site, which needs storage this path does not have (`(module state/model limits; cross-module accessors for writable globals) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted; fixed by cross-module accessors for writable globals)`) — and a BACKEND rule that a module nothing binds needs no library at all, an importer reading the constant directly instead of linking a library for it (`bugs/FORMAL_a_module_that_exports_nothing_cannot_be_a_dylib.md`). Neither exists here, so this is refused at the build rather than linked as a library with nothing in it, and nothing here is waiting on this file.)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/collections/interval.mojo  (build: interval.mojo imports 'std.builtin.string_literal', which cannot be built either: _Global._deinit_wrapper: `dealloc` is called, and it is imported from `std.memory.alloc`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.memory.alloc` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule applied to the DECLARATION: a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation, and each instantiation is the boundary symbol — `formal/monomorph.py` compiles one into the defining module's library when an importer asks for it, and a bare call asks for none. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.dealloc`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `dealloc(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `dealloc[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule applied to the DECLARATION: a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation, and each instantiation is the boundary symbol — `formal/monomorph.py` compiles one into the defining module's library when an importer asks for it, and a bare call asks for none. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/logger/__init__.mojo  (build: __init__.mojo imports '.logger', which cannot be built either: _io.mojo: formal dylib has no public functions: _io.mojo exports nothing under doc/ABI.md's rules because it declares no function and no type at all — only module-level constants, and a constant is not a boundary symbol: there is nothing an importer could bind, so the refusal itself is correct. What would remove it is not a declaration this file is missing, and saying so is the point: the two things that would answer it are a value model for a container-valued module constant — inline it at the use site, which needs storage this path does not have (`(module state/model limits; cross-module accessors for writable globals) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted; fixed by cross-module accessors for writable globals)`) — and a BACKEND rule that a module nothing binds needs no library at all, an importer reading the constant directly instead of linking a library for it (`bugs/FORMAL_a_module_that_exports_nothing_cannot_be_a_dylib.md`). Neither exists here, so this is refused at the build rather than linked as a library with nothing in it, and nothing here is waiting on this file.)
BACKEND-CRASH: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/math/__init__.mojo  (the backend raised: formal.monomorph.MonomorphError: sqrt[SIMD[dtype,width], 1] is a comptime specialization of a FUNCTION template, and a value specialization of a function is a call site binding, not a boundary symbol: both backends already lower it through `mojo/middle/comptime.specialization_args`, so demanding an instantiation here would publish a second copy of a body the build already emits under its own name.  This is not a refusal of the program — the call keeps its brackets and the local machinery answers them)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/io/io.mojo  (build: io.mojo imports 'std._plugin', which cannot be built either: builtin_slice.mojo: is an `Optional[Int]`, and this target has no word to spell `None` as for it: every word is a value of `Int`. An `Optional` here is ONE 64-bit word holding the payload, so `None` has to be a word the payload cannot produce (`formal/model.py`'s `optional_none_word`, which is what answers `Optional[Bool]`, `Optional[String]`, `Optional[Int32]` and every reference-shaped payload from this same table). Answered anyway it would have to treat the integer 0 as the empty one, which is WRONG about `Some(0)` — measured on both architectures: `var z: Optional[Int] = 0; if z is None:` prints the empty branch. So this is refused rather than answered. What closes it is the OTHER representation: a tagged TWO-WORD value, `{tag, payload}`, where `Optional[T]` is the address of the pair — which is what `formal/model.py`'s `OPTIONAL_TWO_WORD` records, with the three measured reasons it is not a one-line change, and which covers every payload type including this one)

CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/origin/__init__.mojo  (build: the module-level comptime binding 'AnyOrigin' is initialized from an MLIR attribute template: __mlir_attr[`#lit.any.origin : !lit.origin<`, +mut._mlir_value, `>`] assembles an MLIR attribute from a template of backtick-quoted literal fragments and compile-time sub-expressions: it is not a subscript, and there is no MLIR on this path for the template to become. Refused rather than materialized as a container, which would turn an attribute into a pointer to a frame blob and disagree with the compiler that does have MLIR. Module-level `comptime` bindings are not part of the function-body expression walk on this path, so nothing used to refuse this and the binding read as an undefined name at every use site.)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/memory/unsafe_pointer.mojo  (build: unsafe_pointer.mojo imports 'std.collections', which cannot be built either: _io.mojo: formal dylib has no public functions: _io.mojo exports nothing under doc/ABI.md's rules because it declares no function and no type at all — only module-level constants, and a constant is not a boundary symbol: there is nothing an importer could bind, so the refusal itself is correct. What would remove it is not a declaration this file is missing, and saying so is the point: the two things that would answer it are a value model for a container-valued module constant — inline it at the use site, which needs storage this path does not have (`(module state/model limits; cross-module accessors for writable globals) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted; fixed by cross-module accessors for writable globals)`) — and a BACKEND rule that a module nothing binds needs no library at all, an importer reading the constant directly instead of linking a library for it (`bugs/FORMAL_a_module_that_exports_nothing_cannot_be_a_dylib.md`). Neither exists here, so this is refused at the build rather than linked as a library with nothing in it, and nothing here is waiting on this file.)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/os/env.mojo  (build: env.mojo imports 'std.ffi', which cannot be built either: _Global._deinit_wrapper: `dealloc` is called, and it is imported from `std.memory.alloc`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.memory.alloc` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule applied to the DECLARATION: a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation, and each instantiation is the boundary symbol — `formal/monomorph.py` compiles one into the defining module's library when an importer asks for it, and a bare call asks for none. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.dealloc`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `dealloc(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `dealloc[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/os/os.mojo  (build: os.mojo imports 'std._plugin', which cannot be built either: builtin_slice.mojo: is an `Optional[Int]`, and this target has no word to spell `None` as for it: every word is a value of `Int`. An `Optional` here is ONE 64-bit word holding the payload, so `None` has to be a word the payload cannot produce (`formal/model.py`'s `optional_none_word`, which is what answers `Optional[Bool]`, `Optional[String]`, `Optional[Int32]` and every reference-shaped payload from this same table). Answered anyway it would have to treat the integer 0 as the empty one, which is WRONG about `Some(0)` — measured on both architectures: `var z: Optional[Int] = 0; if z is None:` prints the empty branch. So this is refused rather than answered. What closes it is the OTHER representation: a tagged TWO-WORD value, `{tag, payload}`, where `Optional[T]` is the address of the pair — which is what `formal/model.py`'s `OPTIONAL_TWO_WORD` records, with the three measured reasons it is not a one-line change, and which covers every payload type including this one)

So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/prelude/__init__.mojo  (build: __init__.mojo imports 'std.collections', which cannot be built either: _io.mojo: formal dylib has no public functions: _io.mojo exports nothing under doc/ABI.md's rules because it declares no function and no type at all — only module-level constants, and a constant is not a boundary symbol: there is nothing an importer could bind, so the refusal itself is correct. What would remove it is not a declaration this file is missing, and saying so is the point: the two things that would answer it are a value model for a container-valued module constant — inline it at the use site, which needs storage this path does not have (`(module state/model limits; cross-module accessors for writable globals) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted; fixed by cross-module accessors for writable globals)`) — and a BACKEND rule that a module nothing binds needs no library at all, an importer reading the constant directly instead of linking a library for it (`bugs/FORMAL_a_module_that_exports_nothing_cannot_be_a_dylib.md`). Neither exists here, so this is refused at the build rather than linked as a library with nothing in it, and nothing here is waiting on this file.)

A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/sys/_amdgpu.mojo  (build: _amdgpu.mojo imports 'std.collections', which cannot be built either: _io.mojo: formal dylib has no public functions: _io.mojo exports nothing under doc/ABI.md's rules because it declares no function and no type at all — only module-level constants, and a constant is not a boundary symbol: there is nothing an importer could bind, so the refusal itself is correct. What would remove it is not a declaration this file is missing, and saying so is the point: the two things that would answer it are a value model for a container-valued module constant — inline it at the use site, which needs storage this path does not have (`(module state/model limits; cross-module accessors for writable globals) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted) (deleted; fixed by cross-module accessors for writable globals)`) — and a BACKEND rule that a module nothing binds needs no library at all, an importer reading the constant directly instead of linking a library for it (`bugs/FORMAL_a_module_that_exports_nothing_cannot_be_a_dylib.md`). Neither exists here, so this is refused at the build rather than linked as a library with nothing in it, and nothing here is waiting on this file.)
CODEGEN: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/sys/arg.mojo  (build: Span[StaticString, ImmStaticOrigin] is a compile-time explicit-parameter list on a generic, not a subscript: the brackets name types and comptime values, none of which is a runtime word. This path has no type or comptime parameter to bind, so what the call means depends entirely on which parameters were passed. Of its 2 element(s): element 1 `StaticString` is a type this image knows; and element 2 `ImmStaticOrigin` is a bare name this image cannot resolve to a declaration. That is where the missing word is. A bare name has two shapes here, and this path cannot tell them apart: a TYPE this unit does not declare, or a `comptime` ALIAS whose initializer does not fold — measured on `std/sys/arg.mojo`'s `Span[StaticString, ImmStaticOrigin]`, whose second element is exactly the second shape, because `std/origin/__init__.mojo` binds it to `Origin[_mlir_attr[…]]()` and that module does not build. The word is missing because of a binding in another module, not because of a capability here. Refused rather than read as an index — a binding for `size_of[type, target]` would have to come from a target description this backend does not have, and a plausible constant is a fabricated answer.)

CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/traits/__init__.mojo  (build: __init__.mojo imports '.copyable', which cannot be built either: Allocation.write_to: `FormatStruct` is called, and it is imported from `std.format._utils`, so the call has to bind a symbol `std.format._utils` exports. That module does not export it, and the reason is `doc/ABI.md`'s export rule applied to the DECLARATION: a generic template is not one symbol but one per instantiation, and each instantiation is the boundary symbol — `formal/monomorph.py` compiles one into the defining module's library when an importer asks for it, and a bare call asks for none. A generic template's instantiations ARE compiled into that module's library when an importer asks for them (`formal/monomorph.py`), so this call is one that asked for none — it names no type argument, or names one that is a value rather than a type, or spells the template as `module.FormatStruct`. **If the call names no type argument at all, the SOURCE is right and this path is short**: Mojo infers a template call's type arguments, so `FormatStruct(…)` with no bracket is correct code — the stdlib's own `FormatStruct(writer, "Allocation")` is spelled that way — and this path infers them only from DECLARED argument types (a written annotation, `x^` of one, a `self.f` field, or a call with a declared `->`), never from a literal or an unwritten type. Its demand pipeline otherwise reads type arguments off an explicit bracket, so this bare call arrives here with no instantiation to bind; the inference, the 123 measured files it is worth, and the shape of the missing piece are in bugs/FORMAL_a_bare_call_to_a_template_whose_type_arguments_are_inferrable.md. So write the operation in this module, or call a public function that does it — which is the same program with a definition this image can bind. Spelling it `FormatStruct[<a type>](…)` will carry the instantiation, and is a workaround for this gap rather than a correction to your code)

Of its 2 element(s): element 2 `True` is a literal that folds; and element 1 `serialize_fn` is a bare name this image cannot resolve to a declaration. That is where the missing word is. CODEGEN/DEPENDENCY: ../new-modular/Mojo/stdlib/std/utils/__init__.mojo  (build: __init__.mojo imports '.index', which cannot be built either: builtin_slice.mojo: is an `Optional[Int]`, and this target has no word to spell `None` as for it: every word is a value of `Int`. An `Optional` here is ONE 64-bit word holding the payload, so `None` has to be a word the payload cannot produce (`formal/model.py`'s `optional_none_word`, which is what answers `Optional[Bool]`, `Optional[String]`, `Optional[Int32]` and every reference-shaped payload from this same table). Answered anyway it would have to treat the integer 0 as the empty one, which is WRONG about `Some(0)` — measured on both architectures: `var z: Optional[Int] = 0; if z is None:` prints the empty branch. So this is refused rather than answered. What closes it is the OTHER representation: a tagged TWO-WORD value, `{tag, payload}`, where `Optional[T]` is the address of the pair — which is what `formal/model.py`'s `OPTIONAL_TWO_WORD` records, with the three measured reasons it is not a one-line change, and which covers every payload type including this one)
